\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha}b_{\beta}
.
Any transverse vector
A^{\mu}
, satisfying
u_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
, admits the
decomposition

A^{\mu}=A_{\parallel}^{\mu}+A_{\perp}^{\mu},\qquad A_{\parallel}^{\mu}\equiv-(A\cdot b)\,b^{\mu},\qquad A_{\perp}^{\mu}\equiv\Xi^{\mu}{}_{\nu}A^{\nu},

(3.54)

with
A\cdot b=A_{\mu}b^{\mu}
. In an isotropic medium the response to

A_{\parallel}^{\mu}
and
A_{\perp}^{\mu}
must be the same, reducing to a
single scalar coefficient. In a magnetized medium they are physically
inequivalent and may carry different coefficients. In addition, the
pseudotensor
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha}b_{\beta}
allows a

Hall-type
contribution. The most general linear relation between
a transverse vector flux and a transverse vector force is therefore

V_{q}^{\mu}=\sigma_{E}^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{E}_{\nu},

(3.55)

with

\sigma_{E}^{\mu\nu}=\sigma_{\perp}\,\Xi^{\mu\nu}-\sigma_{\parallel}\,b^{\mu}b^{\nu}+\sigma_{H}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha}b_{\beta}.

(3.56)

The coefficients
\sigma_{\parallel}
,
\sigma_{\perp}
, and
\sigma_{H}
are
the longitudinal, transverse, and Hall conductivities,
respectively
[
59
,
56
,
53
,
52
]
. The magnetic field splits the response into these
three independent channels, with one coefficient for each sector that
the broken symmetry renders independent.

The same logic applies to the shear-stress tensor. In an isotropic
fluid, the only allowed linear map between the symmetric, traceless,
transverse tensors
\sigma^{\mu\nu}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}
is the
isotropic double projector, giving
\pi^{\mu\nu}=2\eta\,\sigma^{\mu\nu}
.
In a magnetized fluid, the system becomes anisotropic and the most general linear relation between the shear-stress tensor and the shear tensor is,

\pi^{\mu\nu}=2\,\eta^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\sigma_{\alpha\beta},

(3.57)

and the shear viscosity becomes a fourth rank tensor. Expanding
\eta^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}
in the five rank-four projectors
allowed by the residual symmetry around
b^{\mu}
,

\displaystyle\eta^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\|}\left(\frac{\Xi^{\mu\nu}}{2}+b^{\mu}b^{\nu}\right)\left(\frac{\Xi^{\alpha\beta}}{2}+b^{\alpha}b^{\beta}\right)+\eta_{\perp\!\!\!\perp}\Xi^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}+\eta_{\perp}b^{(\mu}\Xi^{\nu)(\alpha}b^{\beta)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\,\eta_{H_{1}}b^{(\mu}b^{\nu)(\beta}b^{\alpha)}+\eta_{H_{2}}\Big[b^{\mu(\alpha}\Xi^{\beta)\nu}+b^{\nu(\alpha}\Xi^{\beta)\nu}\Big],

(3.58)

Above we introduced 5 viscosities that quantify dissipative processes in the system.
\eta_{\|}
,
\eta_{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
and
\eta_{\perp}
describe dissipative processes in combinations of directions parallel and orthogonal to the magnetic field. The coefficients

\eta_{H_{1}}
and
\eta_{H_{2}}
do not describe dissipative processes per se, and are analogous to the Hall conductivities discussed above.
In the limit
B\rightarrow 0
, this decomposition reduces to
\Delta^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}
and we recover the traditional expression for the shear-stress tensor.

3.9
Why Relativistic Navier–Stokes fails

The simplest way to understand why the relativistic Navier–Stokes theory is problematic is to study the diffusion of a scalar density, before any tensor structure or magnetic field enters the picture.
Suppose
\varphi
satisfies a conservation law

\partial_{t}\varphi+\nabla_{i}J^{i}=0,

(3.59)

together with the Fick-type constitutive relation

J^{i}=-D\,\nabla^{i}\varphi.

(3.60)

Substituting (
3.60
) into (
3.59
) yields
the diffusion equation

\partial_{t}\varphi-D\,\nabla^{2}\varphi=0.

(3.61)

This equation is
parabolic
. Its fundamental solution for an initial
delta-function perturbation
\varphi(\mathbf{x},0)=\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{x})

is

\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)=\frac{1}{(4\pi Dt)^{3/2}}\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{4Dt}\right),\qquad t>0.

(3.62)

For any
t>0
, no matter how small,
\varphi(\mathbf{x},t)\neq 0
for all

\mathbf{x}
. A localised perturbation becomes non-zero everywhere
instantaneously. There is no finite propagation speed which means information has
travelled an arbitrarily large distance in an arbitrarily short time.

In a non-relativistic context, this is an idealization that one typically accepts. In a relativistic theory, it is a fundamental violation though. No physical signal should propagate faster than the speed of light
c
. The parabolic structure of the diffusion equation is, therefore, incompatible with special relativity.

This is not a peculiarity of scalar diffusion but reflects a general property of any first-order gradient theory. In relativistic Navier–Stokes theory, the dissipative fluxes, namely, the bulk viscous pressure
\Pi
, the charge diffusion current
V^{\mu}
, and the shear-stress tensor
\pi^{\mu\nu}
, are given
algebraically
as linear functions of first-order gradients of the hydrodynamic fields:

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=-\zeta\,\theta,

(3.63)

\displaystyle V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{n}\nabla^{\mu}\alpha,

(3.64)

\displaystyle\pi^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\eta\,\sigma^{\mu\nu}.

(3.65)

These are not evolution equations for the dissipative variables. They are constraints that hold at every instant. Substituting them back into the conservation equations
\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
and
\partial_{\mu}N^{\mu}=0
produces a system of second-order partial differential equations for the thermodynamic fields
(T,\mu,u^{\mu})
that is, again, parabolic.

The consequences are two-fold and both incompatible with a relativistic theory:

1.

Acausality.
Because the constitutive relations are
algebraic, a gradient anywhere in the fluid instantaneously produces a
dissipative flux everywhere. The propagation speed of perturbations in the
linearised theory is formally infinite. One can verify this explicitly by
performing a Fourier analysis: writing
\varphi\sim e^{-i\omega t+ikx}

and substituting into the linearised equations gives a dispersion relation

\omega=-iDk^{2}+\mathcal{O}(k^{4}),

(3.66)

for the diffusive mode. The phase velocity
v_{\phi}=\omega/k\sim-iDk

grows without bound as
k\to\infty
, confirming that arbitrarily
short-wavelength perturbations travel at arbitrarily large speeds.

2.

Linear instability.
Beyond the issue of propagation speed,
the theory develops exponentially growing modes in the linearised
analysis around a
moving
background. Hiscock and Lindblom
[
57
,
58
]
showed
in 1985 that the first-order Eckart and Landau frames are both linearly unstable for any nonzero background velocity, i.e., small perturbations
grow without bound on short timescales, making any numerical simulation around a moving fluid cell ill-posed. Therefore, this instability is not an artifact of the choice of variables or frame, it remains present no matter which first-order frame is used.

Both issues share the same origin: the dissipative variables have no dynamics of their own. They are dictated by the gradients of the thermodynamic fields at every instant, with no memory and no finite response time. The theory has no mechanism to prevent a sudden gradient from producing an instantaneous, unbounded dissipative response
[
12
,
11
,
43
]
. An alternative causal first-order framework, due to Bemfica, Disconzi, Noronha, and Kovtun (BDNK)
[
12
,
11
]
, achieves causality through a careful out-of-equilibrium frame choice rather than relaxation; in this thesis we follow the Israel–Stewart route instead.

3.10
Israel–Stewart theory

A resolution of the causality problem is conceptually straightforward: one must
give the dissipative variables their own dynamics, allowing them to
relax

toward their Navier–Stokes values over a finite timescale
\tau
rather than
being instantaneously constrained to match them. The simplest illustration is to
replace Fick’s law by the Cattaneo equation
[
22
]
,

\tau\,\partial_{t}J^{i}+J^{i}=-D\,\nabla^{i}\varphi,

(3.67)

which, upon substitution into the conservation law, yields the
telegraph equation
– a
damped wave equation that is hyperbolic with the finite propagation speed

v=\sqrt{\frac{D}{\tau}}.

(3.68)

This is already visible in the dispersion relation of the telegraph equation,

\omega=-\frac{i}{2\tau}\pm\sqrt{\frac{D}{\tau}k^{2}-\frac{1}{4\tau^{2}}},

(3.69)

which at large
k
gives
|\omega/k|\to\sqrt{D/\tau}
, i.e., a finite,
k
-independent
propagation speed, in contrast to the Navier–Stokes result
|\omega/k|\sim Dk\to\infty
.
Thus the presence of relaxation time regulates the ultraviolet behaviour of the dispersion relation and restores causality.

Israel and Stewart
[
62
,
61
]
elevated this idea to the
full relativistic setting by deriving evolution equations for all dissipative
currents from the requirement that an extended entropy current have non-negative
divergence at second order in deviations from equilibrium.

3.10.1
Derivation from the entropy current

In the Navier–Stokes construction, the entropy current was taken in the form

S^{\mu}=su^{\mu}-\alpha V^{\mu},

(3.70)

which leads to constitutive relations that are algebraic in the gradients.
Israel and Stewart proposed that, away from equilibrium, the entropy current should
depend not only on the local equilibrium fields
(\alpha,\beta,u^{\mu})
, but also on
the dissipative quantities themselves
[
62
,
61
]
. Keeping
terms up to second order, one writes
[
61
]

S^{\mu}=su^{\mu}-\alpha V^{\mu}+Q^{\mu},

(3.71)

where
Q^{\mu}
is quadratic in the dissipative variables. The simplest
ansatz
that reduces the entropy density is,

Q^{\mu}=-\frac{u^{\mu}}{2}\left(\beta_{0}\Pi^{2}-\beta_{1}V_{\nu}V^{\nu}+\beta_{2}\pi_{\alpha\beta}\pi^{\alpha\beta}\right),

(3.72)

with
\beta_{0}
,
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
functions of the local equilibrium state. In principle, we could have also included second-order terms that combine dissipative currents, i.e.,
\sim\Pi\,V^{\mu}
and
\sim V_{\nu}\pi^{\mu\nu}
. These terms were omitted for the sake of simplicity and do not affect the following discussion.

Taking the divergence gives

\partial_{\mu}S^{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}(su^{\mu}-\alpha V^{\mu})+\partial_{\mu}Q^{\mu}.

(3.73)

The first term is the first-order entropy production,

\partial_{\mu}(su^{\mu}-\alpha V^{\mu})=-\beta\Pi\theta-V^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\alpha+\beta\pi^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}.

(3.74)

The divergence of
Q^{\mu}
introduces comoving derivatives of the dissipative
currents; using Eq. (
3.72
) one finds schematically

\begin{split}\partial_{\mu}Q^{\mu}={}&-\beta_{0}\Pi D\Pi+\beta_{1}V_{\mu}DV^{\mu}-\beta_{2}\pi_{\mu\nu}D\pi^{\mu\nu}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Pi^{2}D\beta_{0}-V_{\mu}V^{\mu}D\beta_{1}+\pi_{\mu\nu}\pi^{\mu\nu}D\beta_{2}\right)\\
&-\frac{\theta}{2}\left(\beta_{0}\Pi^{2}-\beta_{1}V_{\mu}V^{\mu}+\beta_{2}\pi_{\mu\nu}\pi^{\mu\nu}\right).\end{split}

(3.75)

Unlike in first-order theory, the entropy production now contains the derivatives

D\Pi
,
DV^{\mu}
, and
D\pi^{\mu\nu}
—this is the origin of relaxation dynamics.

Combining Eqs. (
3.74
) and (
3.75
)
and grouping by dissipative flux,

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}S^{\mu}={}

\displaystyle-\Pi\left[\beta\theta+\beta_{0}D\Pi+\tfrac{1}{2}\Pi\partial_{\mu}(\beta_{0}u^{\mu})\right]

\displaystyle\quad-V^{\mu}\left[\nabla_{\mu}\alpha-\beta_{1}\Delta_{\mu\nu}DV^{\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}V_{\mu}\partial_{\lambda}(\beta_{1}u^{\lambda})\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\pi^{\mu\nu}\left[\beta\sigma_{\mu\nu}-\beta_{2}\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{\alpha\beta}D\pi_{\alpha\beta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\pi_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}(\beta_{2}u^{\lambda})\right].

(3.76)

The second law
\partial_{\mu}S^{\mu}\geq 0
is satisfied by requiring that the combined terms inside each bracket must
be proportional to the corresponding dissipative current,

\displaystyle\beta\theta+\beta_{0}D\Pi+\tfrac{1}{2}\Pi\partial_{\mu}(\beta_{0}u^{\mu})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Pi}{\zeta},

(3.77)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\alpha-\beta_{1}\Delta_{\mu\nu}DV^{\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}V_{\mu}\partial_{\lambda}(\beta_{1}u^{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{\mu}}{\kappa_{n}},

(3.78)

\displaystyle\beta\sigma_{\mu\nu}-\beta_{2}\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{\alpha\beta}D\pi_{\alpha\beta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\pi_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}(\beta_{2}u^{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{\mu\nu}}{2\eta},

(3.79)

which recovers the traditional positive semi-definite entropy production,

\partial_{\mu}S^{\mu}=\frac{\Pi^{2}}{\zeta}-\frac{V^{\mu}V_{\mu}}{\kappa_{n}}+\frac{\pi^{\mu\nu}\pi_{\mu\nu}}{2\eta}\geq 0,

(3.80)

provided
\zeta,\kappa_{n},\eta\geq 0
.
Introducing the relaxation times

\tau_{\Pi}\equiv\zeta\beta_{0},\qquad\tau_{n}\equiv\kappa_{n}\beta_{1},\qquad\tau_{\pi}\equiv 2\eta\beta_{2},

(3.81)

one obtains equations for the dissipative
currents in their usual relaxation-type structure
[
62
,
61
,
78
]
,

\displaystyle\tau_{\Pi}D\Pi+\Pi

\displaystyle=-\zeta\theta-\frac{\zeta}{2}\Pi\,\partial_{\mu}\!\left(\frac{\tau_{\Pi}}{\zeta}u^{\mu}\right),

(3.82)

\displaystyle\tau_{n}\Delta^{\mu}_{\ \nu}DV^{\nu}+V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{n}\nabla^{\mu}\alpha-\frac{\kappa_{n}}{2}V^{\mu}\partial_{\lambda}\!\left(\frac{\tau_{n}}{\kappa_{n}}u^{\lambda}\right),

(3.83)

\displaystyle\tau_{\pi}\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}D\pi^{\alpha\beta}+\pi^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\eta\sigma^{\mu\nu}-\eta\,\pi^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\!\left(\frac{\tau_{\pi}}{2\eta}u^{\lambda}\right).

(3.84)

These reduce to the Navier–Stokes constitutive laws in the formal limit

\tau_{\Pi},\tau_{n},\tau_{\pi}\to 0
, but for finite relaxation times describe a
delayed response of the dissipative currents to the thermodynamic forces. Taking this limit is equivalent to neglecting the higher-order contributions to the entropy four-current. These equations can be linearly causal and stable as long as the transport coefficients, in particular the relaxation times, satisfy some basic requirements
[
27
,
36
]
. For instance, for a theory with only shear viscosity, one obtains that the shear viscosity and relaxation time must satisfy the inequality,

c_{s}^{2}+\frac{4\eta}{3\tau_{\pi}\left(\epsilon+P\right)}\leq 1.

(3.85)

A more complete second-order formulation contains additional couplings required by
gradient power counting and kinetic-theory derivations
[
32
,
31
,
13
,
26
,
36
]
.
In the Landau frame
[
79
,
98
,
90
,
80
,
60
,
55
,
99
,
18
]
,

\displaystyle\tau_{\Pi}D\Pi+\Pi

\displaystyle=-\zeta\theta-\delta_{\Pi\Pi}\Pi\theta+\lambda_{\Pi n}n\cdot Du+\lambda_{\Pi\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}+\cdots,

(3.86)

\displaystyle\tau_{n}\Delta^{\mu}_{\ \nu}DV^{\nu}+V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{n}\nabla^{\mu}\alpha-\delta_{nn}V^{\mu}\theta-\tau_{n\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\alpha-\lambda_{n\omega}V_{\nu}\omega^{\nu\mu}+\cdots,

(3.87)

\displaystyle\tau_{\pi}\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}D\pi^{\alpha\beta}+\pi^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\eta\sigma^{\mu\nu}-\delta_{\pi\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}\theta-\tau_{\pi\pi}\pi^{\lambda\langle\mu}\sigma^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \ \lambda}+2\tau_{\pi}\pi^{\lambda\langle\mu}\omega^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \ \lambda}+\lambda_{\pi n}n^{\langle\mu}\nabla^{\nu\rangle}\alpha+\cdots.

(3.88)

The precise values of the second-order transport coefficients depend on the
microscopic theory and approximation
scheme
[
32
,
31
,
13
,
26
,
36
]
.
Israel–Stewart theory thus promotes the dissipative currents from algebraic constraints to dynamical variables, restoring causality. In the next Chapter, we will derive these equations from kinetic theory.

3.11
The Problem in Magnetized Fluids & Summary

The failure of relativistic Navier–Stokes theory comes from the structure of
the first-order theory itself. The dissipative fluxes are fixed algebraically by
local gradients, with no independent dynamics and no finite response time. This
makes the equations parabolic and leads to acausality and instability. The same
issue remains when electromagnetic fields are included.

A magnetic field makes the constitutive relations anisotropic. The diffusion
current splits into longitudinal, transverse, and Hall sectors, while the shear
stress is controlled by a rank-four viscosity tensor. However, the first-order
relations still have the form

V_{q}^{\mu}=\sigma_{E}^{\mu\nu}\left(E_{\nu}-T\nabla_{\nu}\alpha\right),\qquad\pi^{\mu\nu}=2\,\eta^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\sigma_{\alpha\beta}.

(3.89)

Thus the dissipative fluxes are still determined instantaneously by the local
thermodynamic forces. The magnetic field changes the number and tensorial
structure of the transport coefficients, but the first-order equations remain
parabolic. For this reason, the same acausal signal propagation and instability
are expected to persist in relativistic magnetohydrodynamics

[
14
,
8
]
.

In this chapter we showed how this problem can be addressed phenomenologically. We started
from the decomposition of the conserved currents and the entropy-current
analysis, derived the Navier–Stokes constitutive relations, and discussed their
failure in the relativistic regime. We then showed how Israel–Stewart theory
restores causality by promoting the dissipative currents to independent
dynamical variables with their own relaxation equations. The goal of this thesis
is to extend this causal framework to magnetized relativistic fluids, where the
magnetic field opens additional dissipative channels that also require transient
dynamics.

The next chapter develops the microscopic foundation for this construction. So far, the entire framework has been phenomenological: the constitutive relations and relaxation equations were derived by imposing the second law on a postulated entropy current, with no reference to the underlying microscopic dynamics. This changes in the next chapter where we start from the relativistic Boltzmann equation, applying the method of moments, and derive the Israel–Stewart theory from kinetic theory. This provides the microscopic basis on which the magnetized theory is then built in the chapters that follow.

Chapter 4
Microscopic foundations of relativistic hydrodynamics

The macroscopic formulation of relativistic dissipative hydrodynamics introduced in the previous chapter is not yet tied to any particular microscopic dynamics. In order to give microscopic meaning to the conserved currents, dissipative quantities, transport coefficients, and relaxation times, one needs an underlying theory for the dynamics of many particles out of equilibrium. For dilute and moderately dilute systems, that role is played by relativistic kinetic theory
[
28
,
23
]
, in which the state of the system is described in terms of the single-particle distribution function evolving in phase space.

Hydrodynamics emerges from kinetic theory after one identifies the macroscopic fields with low moments of the distribution function and systematically reduces the infinitely many microscopic degrees of freedom to a finite set of slow variables. The aim of this chapter is to construct that connection in a form suited for the later developments of this thesis. We first introduce the relativistic distribution function, the Boltzmann equation, and the macroscopic currents obtained from the moments. We then discuss the collision term, its relation to transition rates and differential cross sections, and the generalization to mixtures of gases. After that, we introduce local equilibrium, matching conditions, and the linearization of the collision operator around equilibrium. This provides the basis for the derivation of transient hydrodynamics through Israel–Stewart theory, the 14-moment approximation, and the method of moments
[
62
,
61
,
31
]
. The chapter closes with a brief discussion of how electromagnetic fields modify the kinetic description and why these effects are treated in detail only in the next chapter.

4.1
Single–particle distribution function and invariant phase space

Relativistic kinetic theory describes a many-particle system in terms of a single-particle local momentum distribution function
[
28
]
. For each particle species
i
in a fluid, one introduces a function

f_{i}(x,k),

(4.1)

defined at the spacetime point
x^{\mu}
and four-momentum
k_{i}^{\mu}
. It specifies how the particles of species
i
are distributed in momentum space at each spacetime point. More precisely,
f_{i}(x,k)
is defined such that

f_{i}(x,k)\,d^{3}x\,d^{3}k

(4.2)

gives the number of particles of species
i
contained in the phase-space cell around
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{k})
.

In the relativistic theory, however, the momentum variables are not independent, since the particle four-momentum is constrained to the positive-energy mass shell,

k_{i}^{\mu}k_{i\mu}=m_{i}^{2},\qquad k_{i}^{0}>0.

(4.3)

Thus, the distribution function is not defined on the full eight-dimensional phase space, but on the seven-dimensional on-shell phase space. In order for the moments of
f_{i}
to transform covariantly,
f_{i}(x,k)
itself must be a Lorentz scalar. It is therefore the relativistic generalization of the ordinary phase-space density used in non-relativistic kinetic theory. The Lorentz-invariant momentum-space measure for species
i
is

dK_{i}\equiv g_{i}\,\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}k_{i}^{0}},

(4.4)

where
g_{i}
is the degeneracy factor. Equivalently, one may write

\int dK_{i}\,(\cdots)=g_{i}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\delta\!\left(k^{\mu}k_{\mu}-m_{i}^{2}\right)\Theta(k^{0})\,(\cdots),

(4.5)

where
\delta\!\left(k^{\mu}k_{\mu}-m_{i}^{2}\right)
is the Dirac delta function, which enforces the on-shell condition
k^{\mu}k_{\mu}=m_{i}^{2}
, and
\Theta(k^{0})
is the Heaviside step function, which restricts the integration to positive-energy states. In this way, the Lorentz-invariant measure is seen explicitly to be confined to the positive-energy mass shell
[
28
]
.

Therefore, the role of the distribution function is that all macroscopic information accessible within kinetic theory is encoded in moments of
f_{i}
. However, for the purposes of hydrodynamics, one does not require the full microscopic content of the distribution function, but only a finite set of its lowest moments, which capture the slow, long-wavelength degrees of freedom relevant for a macroscopic description.

4.2
Macroscopic currents as moments of the distribution function

In kinetic theory the hydrodynamic variables are constructed from moments of the distribution function
[
28
]
. In order to understand this, we first describe how the basic conserved currents, i.e., the particle four-current, the net-charge four-current, and the energy-momentum tensor, are expressed in terms of
f_{i}
. Later in this chapter, these relations will be appropriately derived.

For each particle species one may define the particle four-current

N_{i}^{\mu}\equiv\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}f_{i},

(4.6)

and the total particle current is

N^{\mu}\equiv\sum_{i}N_{i}^{\mu}=\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}f_{i}.

(4.7)

Since in this thesis we only consider elastic collisions, the number of particles will be conserved – this will be demonstrated later in this Chapter. If the species carries electric charge
q_{i}
, the corresponding electric net-charge four-current is

J^{\mu}\equiv\sum_{i}q_{i}N_{i}^{\mu}=\sum_{i}q_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}f_{i}.

(4.8)

Similarly, the energy-momentum tensor of the fluid is given by

T^{\mu\nu}\equiv\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}k_{i}^{\nu}f_{i}.

(4.9)

In addition, one may define the entropy current as
[
28
]

S^{\mu}\equiv-\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}\left[f_{i}\ln f_{i}-a_{i}^{-1}(1-a_{i}f_{i})\ln(1-a_i f_i)\right],

(4.10)

with
a_{i}=1
for fermions,
a_{i}=-1
for bosons, and the classical limit understood as
a_{i}\to 0
. In the Maxwell Boltzmann case this reduces to the simpler expression

S^{\mu}=-\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}\left(f_{i}\ln f_{i}-f_{i}\right).

(4.11)

These currents contain the microscopic information required by hydrodynamics. Their decomposition with respect to the local four-velocity
u^{\mu}
yields the usual hydrodynamic variables. Since in the Landau frame,

n\equiv u_{\mu}N^{\mu},\qquad\epsilon\equiv u_{\mu}u_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu},

while the dissipative parts are

V^{\mu}\equiv\Delta^{\mu}_{\ \nu}N^{\nu},\qquad\Pi\equiv-\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\mu\nu}T^{\mu\nu}-P,\qquad\pi^{\mu\nu}\equiv T^{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}.

Thus, the microscopic meaning of the hydrodynamic decomposition is immediate. The particle density, energy density, diffusion current, bulk viscous pressure, and shear-stress tensor are all moments of the underlying distribution functions. Hydrodynamics amounts to evolving these moments without keeping track of the full phase space dynamics.

The definitions above identify the hydrodynamic variables as moments of the single particle distribution function, but they do not yet specify how those moments evolve in time. To obtain dynamics, one needs an equation of motion for the distribution itself. In relativistic kinetic theory this role is played by the Boltzmann equation, whose moments generate the conservation laws and, after suitable closure, the constitutive dynamics of dissipative hydrodynamics.

4.3
Relativistic Boltzmann equation

4.3.1
Collisionless evolution

In the absence of collisions and external forces, a particle distribution is transported along free streaming trajectories in phase space. In this case the distribution function satisfies

k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{i}(x,k)=0.

(4.12)

This equation states that the distribution is conserved along the worldlines of free particles. It captures ballistic propagation, but it does not contain any mechanism that drives the system toward local equilibrium. Consequently, free streaming generally amplifies momentum space anisotropies rather than reducing them.

Although the collisionless equation is too simple to generate hydrodynamics by itself, it is still useful conceptually. It isolates the purely kinematic part of the phase space evolution and makes clear that equilibration requires interactions among particles.

4.3.2
Boltzmann equation with collisions

When binary interactions are included, the evolution equation becomes the relativistic Boltzmann equation
[
28
,
23
]
,

k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{i}(x,k)=C_{i}[f],

(4.13)

where
C_{i}[f]
is the collision term for species
i
. The left-hand side
describes free streaming in spacetime, while the right-hand side accounts
for the local redistribution of particles in momentum space caused by
microscopic scattering processes. The collision term is therefore the
object that encodes all interaction physics, and is derived from a small set of physical assumptions about
binary collisions, combined with a counting argument for how many
particles enter and leave a given phase-space cell.

a. Setting up the phase-space counting:

Consider a single-particle state
(x,k)
.
In the absence of collisions, particles stream freely and their phase-space density is conserved. When binary interactions are present, this
density changes. Denoting the net rate of change per unit spacetime volume per unit momentum-space volume by

C(x,k)
,
the task is to determine the invariant function
C(x,k)
. The derivation rests on three conditions:

1.

Binary collisions only:
At any one spacetime point, at most two particles interact simultaneously. The process considered is

i(k)+j(k^{\prime})\longleftrightarrow i(p)+j(p^{\prime}).

(4.14)

2.

Molecular chaos (Stosszahlansatz):
The momenta of two
particles about to collide are statistically uncorrelated
[
28
]
. The collision rate per unit spacetime volume with incoming momenta

(k,k^{\prime})
is therefore proportional to the product
f(x,k)\,f(x,k^{\prime})
.

3.

Slow variation:
The distribution function varies slowly on the microscopic length and time scales of the interaction. All distribution functions may therefore be evaluated at the same
spacetime point
x
, and the transition rate carries no
x
-dependence.

b. Loss and gain:

A collision of type (
4.14
) with incoming momenta

(k,k^{\prime})
and outgoing momenta
(p,p^{\prime})
removes a particle from the
state
k
. Applying the Stosszahlansatz and integrating over all possible
collision partners and final states, the number of particles

lost
per unit spacetime volume per unit momentum-space volume around
k
is

C_{\mathrm{loss}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\prime}}{k^{\prime 0}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}p}{p^{0}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}p^{\prime}}{p^{\prime 0}}\,f(x,k)\,f(x,k^{\prime})\,W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}},

(4.15)

where the factor of
1/2
corrects for the double counting of identical final-state pairs
(p,p^{\prime})
and
(p^{\prime},p)
. The quantity
W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}
is the Lorentz-invariant transition rate for the scattering process
k,k^{\prime}\to p,p^{\prime}
. The inverse, or “restituting”, process has initial momenta
(p,p^{\prime})
and final
momenta
(k,k^{\prime})
, thereby adding a particle to the state
k
. Its
contribution, the
gain
per unit spacetime volume per unit momentum-space volume, is

C_{\mathrm{gain}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\prime}}{k^{\prime 0}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}p}{p^{0}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}p^{\prime}}{p^{\prime 0}}\,f(x,p)\,f(x,p^{\prime})\,W_{pp^{\prime}\to kk^{\prime}}.

(4.16)

The net change will be given by the gain term minus the loss term,
C(x,k)=C_{\mathrm{gain}}-C_{\mathrm{loss}}
. Before calculating this, we first discuss the fundamental properties of the transition rate.

c. The transition rate and the differential cross-section:

Further, in the collision term, the quantity
W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}
is the
Lorentz-invariant transition rate
[
28
]
. It gives the
probability per unit spacetime volume that an incoming pair with momenta

(k,k^{\prime})
scatters into an outgoing pair
(p,p^{\prime})
. Being a Lorentz scalar
and required to conserve total four-momentum in every collision, its general
form is

W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}=\chi(s,\Theta_{s})\,\delta^{(4)}(k+k^{\prime}-p-p^{\prime}),

(4.17)

where
\chi(s,\Theta_{s})
is a Lorentz-scalar function of the Mandelstam
variable
s=(k+k^{\prime})^{2}
and the CM scattering angle
\Theta_{s}
, and the
four-dimensional delta function enforces energy-momentum conservation event
by event. The physical content of
\chi
will be identified below by
connecting
W
to the differential cross-section.

To determine this connection, one relates
W
to the transition probability
per unit incident flux. The invariant flux for the two incoming particles is

F=\sqrt{(k_{\mu}k^{\prime\mu})^{2}-m^{4}}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{s(s-4m^{2})},

(4.18)

and the differential cross-section element is formally defined as

\mathrm{d}\sigma=\frac{1}{F}\,W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}p}{p^{0}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}p^{\prime}}{p^{\prime 0}}.

(4.19)

Integrating (
4.19
) over the final states and using the delta
function in (
4.17
), one works in the CM frame
where
\bm{k}+\bm{k}^{\prime}=\bm{0}
, writes

\mathrm{d}^{3}p=|\bm{p}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}|\bm{p}|\,\mathrm{d}\Omega
,
performs the
p^{\prime}
-integration with the spatial delta function, and evaluates
the remaining energy integral using

\delta\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{s}-\sqrt{|\bm{p}|^{2}+m^{2}}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{s}{s-4m^{2}}}\;\delta\!\left(|\bm{p}|-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}\right).

(4.20)

The result is

\int\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega\;\frac{\chi(s,\Theta_{s})}{(2\pi)^{6}\,s},

(4.21)

from which one identifies

\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{\mathrm{d}\Omega}=\frac{\chi(s,\Theta_{s})}{(2\pi)^{6}\,s},

(4.22)

so that
\chi
carries the full microscopic information of the interaction.
Writing
\sigma(s,\Theta_{s})\equiv\mathrm{d}\sigma/\mathrm{d}\Omega
, one has

\chi(s,\Theta_{s})=(2\pi)^{6}\,s\,\sigma(s,\Theta_{s}),

(4.23)

and the transition rate (
4.17
) takes its explicit form

W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}=(2\pi)^{6}\,s\,\sigma(s,\Theta_{s})\,\delta^{(4)}(k+k^{\prime}-p-p^{\prime}).

(4.24)

The total cross-section follows by angular integration,

\sigma_{T}(s)=\frac{2\pi}{\nu}\int\mathrm{d}\Theta_{s}\,\sin\Theta_{s}\,\sigma(s,\Theta_{s}).

(4.25)

Once
\sigma(s,\Theta_{s})
is specified, the entire collision term is
determined.

From the representation (
4.17
) an important
symmetry follows at once. Interchanging the labels of initial and final
states,
(k,k^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(p,p^{\prime})
, leaves both
\chi
(which depends
only on
s
and
\Theta_{s}
) and the delta function unchanged. Therefore,

W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}=W_{pp^{\prime}\to kk^{\prime}}.

(4.26)

This is the
detailed-balance property of the transition rate
. It is a
consequence of the Lorentz scalar structure of
W
alone and no separate
assumption about time-reversal invariance of the interaction is required.

d. Collision term:

Using (
4.26
), we can write down the collision term in its widely used form,

C[f]=\frac{1}{\nu}\sum\int dK^{\prime}\,dP\,dP^{\prime}\;W_{pp^{\prime}\to kk^{\prime}}\left(f_{p}f_{p^{\prime}}-f_{k}f_{k^{\prime}}\right),

(4.27)

where we employed the notation
f_{k}=f(x,k)
. We can also extend this expression to describe a mixture of species, and, furthermore, include the effects of quantum statistics. We introduce the factors
\tilde{f}_{i}\equiv 1-a_{i}f_{i}
(with
a_{i}=+1
for fermions,
a_{i}=-1
for bosons, and
a_{i}=0
in the classical limit) that describe the fermi blocking or the boson enhancement effects for the outgoing states. In this case, the
collision term for species
i
takes the form

C_{i}[f]=\frac{1}{\nu_{j}}\sum_{j}\int dK^{\prime}_{j}\,dP_{i}\,dP^{\prime}_{j}\;W_{pp^{\prime}\to kk^{\prime}}\left(f_{p_{i}}f_{p^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{k_{i}}\tilde{f}_{k^{\prime}_{j}}-f_{k_{i}}f_{k^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{p_{i}}\tilde{f}_{p^{\prime}_{j}}\right),

(4.28)

where
\nu_{j}
is the symmetry factor for identical particles and

dK_{i}\equiv g_{i}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}k/[(2\pi)^{3}k^{0}]
is the invariant
phase-space measure. The second term is the loss and the first is the
gain. Collisions do not create or destroy particles; they transfer
occupation between momentum cells. The competition between the two terms
is precisely what drives the system toward local equilibrium.

e. Collision invariants and conservation laws:

For species
i
, the Boltzmann equation reads

k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{i}(x,k)=C_{i}[f].

(4.29)

Taking the zeroth moment means integrating this equation over momentum. More generally, for a weight
\psi_{i}(k)
, we consider

\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,\psi_{i}(k)\,k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{i}=\sum_{i}\partial_{\mu}\left(\int dK_{i}\,\psi_{i}(k)\,k_{i}^{\mu}f_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,\psi_{i}(k)\,C_{i}[f].

(4.30)

The second equality follows since the momentum variables are independent of the spacetime derivative. Thus, each choice of
\psi_{i}(k)
generates an equation of motion for a corresponding moment of the distribution function.

The structure of
C_{i}[f]
encodes a fundamental property. If a quantity

\psi_{i}(k)
is conserved in every individual collision,

\psi_{i}(k)+\psi_{j}(k^{\prime})=\psi_{i}(p)+\psi_{j}(p^{\prime}),

(4.31)

then it is a
collision invariant
and satisfies

\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,\psi_{i}(k)\,C_{i}[f]=0.

(4.32)

To see this, multiply (
4.28
) by
\psi_{i}(k)
,
sum over species, and integrate over
k
. After relabelling integration
variables
(k,k^{\prime})\leftrightarrow(p,p^{\prime})
in the gain term and
symmetrising between incoming and outgoing momenta, one finds

\begin{split}&\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,\psi_{i}(k)\,C_{i}[f]\\
&\quad=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{ij}\int dK_{i}\,dK^{\prime}_{j}\,dP_{i}\,dP^{\prime}_{j}\;W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}\left[\psi_{i}(k)+\psi_{j}(k^{\prime})-\psi_{i}(p)-\psi_{j}(p^{\prime})\right]\\
&\qquad\times\left(f_{p_{i}}f_{p^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{k_{i}}\tilde{f}_{k^{\prime}_{j}}-f_{k_{i}}f_{k^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{p_{i}}\tilde{f}_{p^{\prime}_{j}}\right).\end{split}

(4.33)

The bracket in square parentheses vanishes identically when
\psi_{i}
satisfies (
4.31
), and (
4.32
) follows immediately. Note that the equation above clearly shows that
1
is a collision invariant. This is not a general result; it happened because of our underlying assumption of elastic collisions.

The most important collision invariants are the conserved charges and
the four-momentum. As already mentioned,
1
is also a collision invariant in our case. If we then consider
\psi_{i}(k)=1
in Eq. (
4.30
), it then becomes

\partial_{\mu}\left(\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}f_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,C_{i}[f]=0.

(4.34)

Using the definition of the total particle current,

N^{\mu}\equiv\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}f_{i},

(4.35)

this may be written as the traditional continuity equation related to the conservation of the number of particles

\partial_{\mu}N^{\mu}=0.

(4.36)

This equation also holds for individual particle species, that is, the number of particles per species is conserved. Naturally, if this is the case, the net-charge currents will also satisfy conservation laws of their own. For instance, the net-charge current will naturally satisfy,

\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}=0.

(4.37)

Likewise, to obtain energy-momentum conservation, we consider the collision invariant
\psi_{i}(k)=k_{i}^{\nu}
and replace it in Eq. (
4.30
). This leads to,

\partial_{\mu}\left(\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}k_{i}^{\nu}f_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\nu}C_{i}[f]=0.

(4.38)

Recognizing the energy-momentum tensor as

T^{\mu\nu}\equiv\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}k_{i}^{\nu}f_{i},

(4.39)

one obtains the continuity equation related to energy-momentum conservation already highlighted several times in this thesis,

\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0.

(4.40)

These relations are exact consequences of the conservation laws on a microscopic level. These analyses demonstrate that the conserved currents are moments of single-particle distribution function, with weights given by collision invariants. Higher moments, involving additional powers of momentum, are not protected by collision invariance and therefore have non-vanishing collision integrals. These moments describe dissipative relaxation and provide the microscopic origin of viscous hydrodynamics.

f. The equilibrium distribution:

Having established what
C_{i}[f]
is and what it conserves, it is
natural to ask: which distribution makes it vanish? A stationary
collisional state satisfies

C_{i}[f_{0}]=0.

(4.41)

For the integrand of (
4.28
) to vanish for
arbitrary momenta and an arbitrary transition rate, one needs

f_{0,p_{i}}\,f_{0,p^{\prime}_{j}}\,\tilde{f}_{0,k_{i}}\,\tilde{f}_{0,k^{\prime}_{j}}=f_{0,k_{i}}\,f_{0,k^{\prime}_{j}}\,\tilde{f}_{0,p_{i}}\,\tilde{f}_{0,p^{\prime}_{j}}.

(4.42)

Taking the logarithm of both sides,

\ln\frac{f_{0,k_{i}}}{\tilde{f}_{0,k_{i}}}+\ln\frac{f_{0,k^{\prime}_{j}}}{\tilde{f}_{0,k^{\prime}_{j}}}=\ln\frac{f_{0,p_{i}}}{\tilde{f}_{0,p_{i}}}+\ln\frac{f_{0,p^{\prime}_{j}}}{\tilde{f}_{0,p^{\prime}_{j}}}.

(4.43)

The quantity
\phi_{i}(k)\equiv\ln(f_{0,i}/\tilde{f}_{0,i})
therefore
satisfies the same additive conservation law as a collision invariant.
Since the only additive invariants of binary collisions conserving charge
and four-momentum are linear combinations of
q_{i}
and
k^{\mu}
, one must
have

\phi_{i}(k)=\alpha_{i}(x)-\beta_{\mu}(x)\,k^{\mu},

(4.44)

where
\alpha_{i}(x)
and
\beta^{\mu}(x)
are spacetime-dependent
coefficients. Inverting the definition of
\phi_{i}
, one obtains the
local equilibrium distribution
[
64
,
28
]
,

f_{0,i}(x,k)=\frac{1}{\exp\!\left[\beta_{\mu}(x)\,k^{\mu}-\alpha_{i}(x)\right]+a_{i}}.

(4.45)

Here
\beta^{\mu}=u^{\mu}/T
is the inverse-temperature four-vector and

\alpha_{i}=\mu_{i}/T
is the thermal potential for species
i
. In the
classical Maxwell–Boltzmann limit
a_{i}\to 0
,
(
4.45
) reduces to

f_{0,i}(x,k)=\exp\!\left[\alpha_{i}(x)-\beta_{\mu}(x)\,k^{\mu}\right],\qquad\ln f_{0,i}=\alpha_{i}-\beta_{\mu}k^{\mu}.

(4.46)

This is the central result. The equilibrium distribution is not an
ansatz; it is the
unique
solution to
C_{i}[f_{0}]=0
given that
binary collisions conserve charge and four-momentum.
Equation (
4.42
) is satisfied identically for
(
4.45
), which is therefore the
kinetic statement of detailed balance.

4.3.3
Local equilibrium and matching conditions

The equilibrium distribution derived above is a solution to
C_{i}[f_{0}]=0
,
but a truly
global
equilibrium state must simultaneously satisfy the
collisionless streaming equation,
k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{i}=0
. Together,
these two requirements force the fields
T
,
\mu_{i}
, and
u^{\mu}
to be
uniform constants throughout spacetime. Such a state is static,
homogeneous, and carries no currents: it is the thermodynamic equilibrium
of an isolated system. While it is an exact solution of the full Boltzmann
equation, it is clearly too restrictive to describe a real fluid undergoing
flow, diffusion, or heat transport.

The concept of
local equilibrium
relaxes this constraint in a
physically motivated way. One retains the functional form of
(
4.45
) but allows the thermodynamic
fields to vary slowly in spacetime,

T=T(x),\quad\mu_{i}=\mu_{i}(x),\quad u^{\mu}=u^{\mu}(x).

(4.47)

The resulting distribution
f_{0i}(x,k)
is isotropic in the local rest
frame at each spacetime point and makes the collision term vanish,

C_{i}[f_{0i}]=0
, pointwise. However, it does
not
satisfy the full
Boltzmann equation, because the streaming term
k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{0i}
no longer vanishes when the fields carry spacetime gradients. Local
equilibrium is therefore not an exact solution of the kinetic equation; it
is an idealized reference state around which the true non-equilibrium
distribution is expanded. It is this expansion that connects kinetic
theory to dissipative hydrodynamics.

Hydrodynamics assumes that the system remains close to this local equilibrium
state, characterized by a small number of slowly varying fields
[
28
,
61
]
. In kinetic theory this is implemented by writing

f_{i}=f_{0i}+\delta f_{i},

(4.48)

where
f_{0i}
is the local equilibrium distribution and
\delta f_{i}
is the off-equilibrium correction.

The local equilibrium distribution is

f_{0i}(x,k)=\left[\exp\left(\beta(x)u(x)\cdot k_{i}-\alpha_{i}(x)\right)+a_{i}\right]^{-1},

(4.49)

with

\beta\equiv\frac{1}{T},\qquad\alpha_{i}\equiv\frac{\mu_{i}}{T},

(4.50)

with
\mu_{i}
being the chemical potential of the species
i
and
T
the total temperature of the system. Because
f_{0i}
depends on momentum only through the scalar
u\cdot k_{i}
, it is isotropic in the local rest frame. Substituting
f_{0i}
into the kinetic definitions of the conserved currents then yields the ideal fluid forms,

N_{(0)}^{\mu}=nu^{\mu},\qquad T_{(0)}^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon u^{\mu}u^{\nu}-P\Delta^{\mu\nu}.

The split in Eq. (
4.48
) is not unique until one fixes the local equilibrium state associated with the full non-equilibrium distribution. This is done through matching conditions
[
61
,
32
]
. In the Landau frame, the local energy density and conserved charge density of the exact state are required to equal those of the associated equilibrium state. Microscopically this means

u_{\mu}u_{\nu}\,\delta T^{\mu\nu}=0,\qquad u_{\mu}\,\delta N^{\mu}=0,

(4.51)

where

\delta N^{\mu}\equiv\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}\delta f_{i},\qquad\delta T^{\mu\nu}\equiv\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\mu}k_{i}^{\nu}\delta f_{i}.

(4.52)

These conditions determine the hydrodynamic fields
T
,
\mu_{i}
, and
u^{\mu}
associated with a given non-equilibrium state. They are the microscopic implementation of the matching procedure already discussed at the macroscopic level in the previous chapter.

Once the matching is fixed, the dissipative quantities acquire direct kinetic expressions. In the Landau frame,

\displaystyle V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\langle\mu\rangle}\delta f_{i},

(4.53)

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,\Delta_{\alpha\beta}k_{i}^{\alpha}k_{i}^{\beta}\,\delta f_{i},

(4.54)

\displaystyle\pi^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int dK_{i}\,k_{i}^{\langle\mu}k_{i}^{\nu\rangle}\delta f_{i}.

(4.55)

Thus, diffusion, bulk pressure, and shear correspond to specific momentum-space distortions of the local equilibrium distribution. These expressions make clear that computing the dissipative corrections reduces to determining
\delta f_{i}
, the off-equilibrium part of the distribution function. To obtain
\delta f_{i}
systematically, one must go back to the Boltzmann equation itself and study how it constrains the evolution of deviations from local equilibrium. This is the subject of the following subsection.

4.3.4
Linearization of the collision operator

Since
\delta f_{i}
is the key quantity governing all dissipative phenomena, the natural next step is to derive an equation it must satisfy. The starting point is the full Boltzmann equation (
4.13
). Because local equilibrium sets the collision term to zero but not the streaming term, substituting the decomposition
f_{i}=f_{0i}+\delta f_{i}
into the Boltzmann equation yields a non-trivial balance. The streaming of
f_{0i}
through an inhomogeneous fluid, which acts as a source, is compensated by the collisional relaxation of
\delta f_{i}
back toward equilibrium. To make this precise, one considers states close to local equilibrium and expands the collision term to first order in
\delta f_{i}

[
28
,
23
]
. Accordingly, one writes

f_{i}=f_{0i}+\delta f_{i},

(4.56)

with
\delta f_{i}
assumed to be small compared to
f_{0i}
. Now, we know that at equilibrium, the distribution can be written as

f_{0i}=\frac{1}{e^{y_{0i}}+a_{i}},\qquad y_{0i}\equiv\beta_{\mu}k_{i}^{\mu}-\alpha_{i},

(4.57)

where
a_{i}=1,-1,0
for fermions, bosons, and in the classical limit, respectively. Away from equilibrium, one may still write the exact distribution in the same form,

f_{i}=\frac{1}{e^{y_{i}}+a_{i}},

(4.58)

but now considering that the variable
y_{i}
may deviate from its equilibrium form,

y_{i}=y_{0i}+\delta y_{i},

(4.59)

where
\delta y_{i}
is small compared to
y_{0}i
. Expanding to first order in
\delta y_{i}
, one obtains

\delta f_{i}=\left.\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial y_{i}}\right|_{y_{i}=y_{0i}}\delta y_{i}.

(4.60)

Since

\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial y_{i}}=-\frac{e^{y_{i}}}{(e^{y_{i}}+a_{i})^{2}}=-f_{i}(1-a_{i}f_{i}),

(4.61)

it follows that, to linear order,

\delta f_{i}=-f_{0i}(1-a_{i}f_{0i})\,\delta y_{i}.

(4.62)

Defining

\phi_{i}\equiv-\delta y_{i},

(4.63)

one arrives at the convenient parametrization

\delta f_{i}=f_{0i}\tilde{f}_{0i}\,\phi_{i},\qquad\tilde{f}_{0i}\equiv 1-a_{i}f_{0i},

(4.64)

so that

f_{i}=f_{0i}\left(1+\tilde{f}_{0i}\phi_{i}\right).

(4.65)

Thus, this form is simply the linear expansion of the exact quantum-statistical distribution around local equilibrium.

The collision term for binary elastic collisions has the form

C_{i}[f]=\frac{1}{\nu_{j}}\sum_{j}\int dK^{\prime}_{j}\,dP_{i}\,dP^{\prime}_{j}\,W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}\left(f_{p_{i}}f_{p^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{k_{i}}\tilde{f}_{k^{\prime}_{j}}-f_{k_{i}}f_{k^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{p_{i}}\tilde{f}_{p^{\prime}_{j}}\right).

(4.66)

To linearize this expression, we substitute Eq. (
4.65
) into the gain and loss terms and expand to first order in
\phi
. Using that
C_{i}[f_{0}]=0
and, thus, that the equilibrium distribution satisfies the detailed balance relation,

f_{0p_{i}}f_{0p^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{0k_{i}}\tilde{f}_{0k^{\prime}_{j}}=f_{0k_{i}}f_{0k^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{0p_{i}}\tilde{f}_{0p^{\prime}_{j}}.

(4.67)

Carrying out the algebra, one obtains the following expression,

C_{i}[f]=\hat{L}_{i}[\phi]+\mathcal{O}(\phi^{2}),

(4.68)

with the linearized collision operator given by

\hat{L}_{i}[\phi]=\frac{1}{\nu_{j}}\sum_{j}\int dK^{\prime}_{j}\,dP_{i}\,dP^{\prime}_{j}\,W_{kk^{\prime}\to pp^{\prime}}\,f_{0k_{i}}f_{0k^{\prime}_{j}}\tilde{f}_{0p_{i}}\tilde{f}_{0p^{\prime}_{j}}\left(\phi_{p_{i}}+\phi_{p^{\prime}_{j}}-\phi_{k_{i}}-\phi_{k^{\prime}_{j}}\right).

(4.69)

This is the basic result of the linearization procedure. It shows that, near equilibrium, the collision term depends linearly on the combination

\phi_{p_{i}}+\phi_{p^{\prime}_{j}}-\phi_{k_{i}}-\phi_{k^{\prime}_{j}},

(4.70)

which measures the extent to which the non-equilibrium correction fails to be conserved across a microscopic collision.

The structure of Eq. (
4.69
) immediately reveals the special role of collision invariants. If
\phi_{i}
is of the form

\phi_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\beta_{\mu}k_{i}^{\mu},

(4.71)

then

\phi_{p_{i}}+\phi_{p^{\prime}_{j}}-\phi_{k_{i}}-\phi_{k^{\prime}_{j}}=0,

(4.72)

because charge and four-momentum are conserved in each binary collision. Therefore such distortions are zero modes of the linearized collision operator,

\hat{L}_{i}[\phi_{\mathrm{inv}}]=0.

(4.73)

These are precisely the modes associated with the conserved quantities, and so they cannot relax through collisions. All other distortions are not protected in this way and are damped by the action of
\hat{L}_{i}
. In other words, the conserved modes appear as zero modes of
\hat{L}_{i}
, while the non-conserved distortions relax with rates determined by the spectrum of the operator.

The linearized Boltzmann equation therefore takes the form

k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{0i}+k_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\delta f_{i}=\hat{L}_{i}[\phi]+\mathcal{O}(\phi^{2}).

(4.74)

It is the starting
point for deriving relativistic hydrodynamics from microscopic dynamics. The next step is to extract the macroscopic consequences of this kinetic equation. This is done by taking moments of the Boltzmann equation with weights corresponding to collision invariants, precisely the procedure established in paragraph
4.3.2
d
. In this way, the conservation laws of hydrodynamics emerge directly from the microscopic dynamics, and the dissipative sector is organized through the Israel–Stewart construction
[
62
,
61
]
, in which the dissipative quantities themselves are promoted to independent dynamical variables and the hierarchy of moment equations is closed through the 14-moment approximation.

4.4
Israel Stewart theory and the 14-moment approximation

One approach to dissipative hydrodynamics is to promote the dissipative quantities themselves to independent dynamical variables
[
62
,
61
]
. This is the basic idea behind Israel–Stewart theory. From the kinetic point of view, it is implemented by expanding the non-equilibrium correction
\delta f_{i}
in a finite set of moments associated with the hydrodynamic degrees of freedom. Historically, this construction is the relativistic extension of Grad’s moment method
[
47
]
, in which the distribution function is approximated by a finite polynomial expansion in momentum around local equilibrium. In this section, we shall consider a single component gas for the sake of simplicity. For this reason, the particle species index
i
shall be omitted from now on.

4.4.1
The 14-moment approximation

The formulation of relativistic fluid dynamics from kinetic theory requires a controlled approximation of the single-particle distribution function away from equilibrium. In general, the non-equilibrium correction encodes an infinite number of microscopic degrees of freedom, making an exact treatment impractical. A systematic reduction becomes possible, however, when the system remains sufficiently close to local equilibrium, so that only a limited set of slow, long-wavelength modes governs the dynamics.

This idea can be traced back to the seminal work of Harold Grad
[
47
]
, who introduced a moment expansion method to approximate the distribution function in non-relativistic gases. Grad’s approach replaces the full momentum dependence by a finite set of moments, effectively projecting the kinetic description onto a small number of macroscopic variables. The 14-moment approximation
[
61
]
builds precisely on this philosophy. It assumes that near equilibrium the full momentum dependence of the deviation
\delta f
need not be retained explicitly, but can instead be represented through a truncated expansion in momentum space. The truncation is chosen such that it captures all hydrodynamic degrees of freedom relevant for a system with a single conserved charge, while remaining minimal.

In this setting, the slow variables consist of the five equilibrium fields—energy density, conserved charge density, and the three independent components of the flow velocity—supplemented by the dissipative quantities: the bulk viscous pressure, the diffusion current, and the shear-stress tensor. Together, these account for fourteen independent moments, which gives the approximation its name and defines the minimal closure consistent with relativistic dissipative hydrodynamics.
The number 14 arises from a simple counting argument. The local
equilibrium state is specified by five fields: the energy density, one
conserved charge density, and the three independent components of

u^{\mu}
. Out of equilibrium, the dissipative corrections introduce
nine additional degrees of freedom,

\underbrace{1}_{\Pi}+\underbrace{3}_{V^{\mu}}+\underbrace{5}_{\pi^{\mu\nu}}=9,

(4.75)

so that the total number of macroscopic degrees of freedom is

5+9=14.

(4.76)

The 14-moment approximation consists in retaining exactly these 14
degrees of freedom in
\delta f_{k}
and setting all remaining moments to
zero.

The 14-moment ansatz:

The starting point is to write the distribution function as

f_{k}=\left[\exp(-y_k)+a\right]^{-1},

(4.77)

where
a=1,-1,0
for fermions, bosons, and a classical gas, respectively.
In local equilibrium,
y_{k}
reduces to
y_{0k}=\alpha_{0}-\beta_{0}E_{k}
, so
the entire deviation from equilibrium is encoded in

\delta y_{k}\equiv y_{k}-y_{0k}
. Linearizing (
4.77
) in

\delta y_{k}
yields

\delta f_{k}\equiv f_{k}-f_{0k}=f_{0k}\tilde{f}_{0k}\,\delta y_{k}+\mathcal{O}(\delta y_{k}^{2}).

(4.78)

In the original Israel–Stewart approach,
\delta y_{k}
is then expanded in a Taylor series in the particle 4-momentum
[
62
,
61
]
,

\delta y_{k}=\varepsilon+k^{\mu}\varepsilon_{\mu}+k^{\mu}k^{\nu}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}+k^{\mu}k^{\nu}k^{\lambda}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\lambda}+\cdots.

(4.79)

The expansion is formally exact but
contains infinitely many unknown coefficients

\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{\mu},\varepsilon_{\mu\nu},\dots
.

Inserting (
4.78
) and (
4.79
) into the kinetic
definitions of
N^{\mu}
(
4.7
) and
T^{\mu\nu}
(
4.9
) gives

N^{\mu}=I_{0}^{\mu}+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\mu}+\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\mu\nu}\varepsilon_{\nu}+\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\mu\nu\lambda}\varepsilon_{\nu\lambda}+\cdots,

(4.80)

T^{\mu\nu}=I_{0}^{\mu\nu}+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\mu\nu\lambda}\varepsilon_{\lambda}+\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}\varepsilon_{\lambda\rho}+\cdots,

(4.81)

where the equilibrium tensors are defined as

I_{0}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}\equiv\int dK\,k^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots k^{\alpha_{n}}\,f_{0k},\qquad\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}\equiv\int dK\,k^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots k^{\alpha_{n}}\,f_{0k}\tilde{f}_{0k}.

(4.82)

The first term in each of (
4.80
) and
(
4.81
) is the ideal-fluid contribution,

N^{\mu}_{\mathrm{ideal}}=I_{0}^{\mu},\qquad T^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{ideal}}=I_{0}^{\mu\nu};

(4.83)

the rest are dissipative corrections. Since
I_{0}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}

and
\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}
are moments of the isotropic
equilibrium distribution, they can be written entirely in terms of
u^{\mu}

and
\Delta^{\mu\nu}
, with scalar coefficients given by the thermodynamic
integrals
\mathcal{I}_{nq}
and
\mathcal{J}_{nq}

[
28
]
.

The 14-moment approximation truncates the Taylor series (
4.79
)
at second order in the 4-momentum, keeping only the tensors
1
,
k^{\mu}
, and

k^{\mu}k^{\nu}
,

\delta y_{k}\approx\varepsilon+k^{\mu}\varepsilon_{\mu}+k^{\mu}k^{\nu}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}.

(4.84)

Without loss of generality,
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}
may be taken symmetric and
traceless: any antisymmetric part vanishes when contracted with

k^{\mu}k^{\nu}
, and any trace can always be absorbed into the scalar

\varepsilon
. The truncated expansion thus carries
1+4+9=14

independent unknowns in
\{\varepsilon,\,\varepsilon_{\mu},\,\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}\}
,
matching exactly the 14 components of
N^{\mu}
and
T^{\mu\nu}
.

Matching procedure:

The 14 expansion coefficients can be uniquely related to the 14 components of
N^{\mu}
and
T^{\mu\nu}
through the so-called
matching
procedure

[
61
,
28
]
. In the Landau frame, the
relevant constraints are

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu\nu}\,N^{\nu}

\displaystyle=V_{\mu},

(4.85)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\,T^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\pi^{\mu\nu},

(4.86)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\mu\nu}\!\left(T^{\mu\nu}-T^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{ideal}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Pi,

(4.87)

\displaystyle u_{\mu}\!\left(N^{\mu}-N^{\mu}_{\mathrm{ideal}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

(4.88)

\displaystyle u_{\nu}\!\left(T^{\mu\nu}-T^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{ideal}}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(4.89)

The first three define the dissipative currents as projections of
N^{\mu}

and
T^{\mu\nu}
, while the last two enforce the Landau matching conditions
(
4.51
) on the energy and charge densities.
Together, they form a system of 14 equations for the 14 unknowns.

Lorentz covariance dictates the most general form the coefficients can
take. The only rank-0, rank-1, and symmetric traceless rank-2 tensors that
can be built from
u^{\mu}
,
\Delta^{\mu\nu}
,
\Pi
,
V^{\mu}
, and

\pi^{\mu\nu}
are

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle=E_{0}\,\Pi,

(4.90)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=D_{0}\,u_{\lambda}\,\Pi+D_{1}\,V_{\lambda},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\lambda\rho}

\displaystyle=B_{0}\!\left(\Delta_{\lambda\rho}-3u_{\lambda}u_{\rho}\right)\Pi+B_{1}\,u_{(\lambda}V_{\rho)}+B_{2}\,\pi_{\lambda\rho},

where
E_{0}
,
D_{0}
,
D_{1}
,
B_{0}
,
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
are six scalar thermodynamic
functions to be determined.

Inserting the ansatz (
4.90
) into the matching equations
(
4.85
)–(
4.89
) yields a closed linear system
for the six scalar coefficients
[
61
,
28
]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{21}D_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{31}B_{1}

\displaystyle=-1,\qquad

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{J}_{42}B_{2}

\displaystyle=1,

(4.91)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{21}E_{0}+\mathcal{J}_{31}D_{0}-(3\mathcal{J}_{41}+5\mathcal{J}_{42})B_{0}

\displaystyle=1,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{10}E_{0}+\mathcal{J}_{20}D_{0}-3(\mathcal{J}_{30}+\mathcal{J}_{31})B_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{31}D_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{41}B_{1}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{20}E_{0}+\mathcal{J}_{30}D_{0}-3(\mathcal{J}_{40}+\mathcal{J}_{41})B_{0}

\displaystyle=0.

The first three equations come from the dissipative-current definitions
(
4.85
)–(
4.87
), and the last three from the
Landau matching conditions (
4.88
)–(
4.89
). The
solution is
[
61
,
28
]

\displaystyle\frac{E_{0}}{3B_{0}}

\displaystyle=m^{2}+4\,\frac{\mathcal{J}_{31}\mathcal{J}_{30}-\mathcal{J}_{41}\mathcal{J}_{20}}{\mathcal{D}_{20}}\equiv-\mathcal{C}_{1},\qquad\frac{D_{0}}{3B_{0}}=-4\,\frac{\mathcal{J}_{31}\mathcal{J}_{20}-\mathcal{J}_{41}\mathcal{J}_{10}}{\mathcal{D}_{20}}\equiv-\mathcal{C}_{2},

(4.92)

\displaystyle B_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathcal{J}_{21}+3\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathcal{J}_{31}+3\mathcal{J}_{41}+5\mathcal{J}_{42}},\quad B_{1}=\frac{\mathcal{J}_{31}}{\mathcal{D}_{31}},\quad D_{1}=-\frac{\mathcal{J}_{41}}{\mathcal{D}_{31}},\quad B_{2}=\frac{1}{2\mathcal{J}_{42}}

(4.93)

with
\mathcal{D}_{20}\equiv\mathcal{J}_{10}\mathcal{J}_{30}-\mathcal{J}_{20}^{2}

and
\mathcal{D}_{31}\equiv\mathcal{J}_{21}\mathcal{J}_{41}-\mathcal{J}_{31}^{2}
.
The numerical values of all six coefficients depend on the equation of state
and on the particle statistics through the integrals
\mathcal{J}_{nq}
defined as

\mathcal{J}_{ij}^{\pm}=\frac{(-1)^{j}}{(2j+1)!!}\int dK\,E_{k}^{\,i-2j}\left(\Delta_{\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha}k^{\beta}\right)^{j}f_{0k}^{\pm}\tilde{f}_{0k}^{\pm}.

(4.94)

This simplifies further in the classical limit as

\mathcal{I}_{ij}^{\pm}=\frac{(-1)^{j}}{(2j+1)!!}\int dK\,E_{k}^{\,i-2j}\left(\Delta_{\alpha\beta}k^{\alpha}k^{\beta}\right)^{j}f_{0k}^{\pm}.

(4.95)

The 14-moment distribution function:

It is convenient to re-express the truncated moment expansion for
\phi_{k}
explicitly in terms of the dissipative currents. In this case, it becomes,

\phi_{k}=\mathcal{A}(E_{k})\,\Pi+\mathcal{B}(E_{k})\,k^{\langle\mu\rangle}V_{\mu}+\mathcal{C}(E_{k})\,k^{\langle\mu}k^{\nu\rangle}\pi_{\mu\nu},

(4.96)

where we defined the energy in the local rest frame,
E_{k}\equiv u_{\mu}k^{\mu}
. The energy dependence of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
is
polynomial in
E_{k}
(at most quadratic in
E_{k}
), as expected from a truncation at second order in momentum.

The decisive feature of (
4.96
) is that
\delta f_{k}

depends on the macroscopic dynamics only through
\Pi
,
V^{\mu}
, and

\pi^{\mu\nu}
. In order to see this, we define general irreducible moments of
f_{k}
as
[
31
]

\rho_{r}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}\equiv\int dK\,E_{k}^{r}\,k^{\langle\mu_{1}}\cdots k^{\mu_{\ell}\rangle}\,\delta f_{k},

(4.97)

where
r
is a non-negative integer labelling the power of
E_{k}

included in the weight. The brackets in the indices of
k^{\langle\mu_{1}}\cdots k^{\mu_{\ell}\rangle}
denote the traceless and symmetric projection orthogonal to the four-velocity. They become proportional to spherical harmonics in the local rest frame of the fluid and thus correspond to a complete and orthogonal basis on
S^{\mathfrak{2}}
. Thus each moment
\rho_{r}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}
is
symmetric and traceless in its free indices and orthogonal to
u^{\mu}
. We note that the
full collection
\{\rho_{r}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}\}
over all
r
and

\ell
contains all the information of the full non-equilibrium correction
\delta f_{k}
, that is,
knowing all moments is equivalent to knowing the distribution function
itself.

The 14-moment approximation leads to a dramatic simplification of this collection of fields. Every irreducible moment of the
distribution function of rank 0, 1, and 2 becomes proportional to one of these dissipative
currents,

\rho_{r}=a_{r}\,\Pi,\qquad\rho_{r}^{\mu}=b_{r}\,V^{\mu},\qquad\rho_{r}^{\mu\nu}=c_{r}\,\pi^{\mu\nu},

(4.98)

with coefficients
a_{r}
,
b_{r}
,
c_{r}
given by simple equilibrium momentum
integrals, while moments of rank
\ell\geq 3
vanish identically. Therefore, this infinite number of degrees of freedom is replaced by the dissipative quantities,
\Pi
,
V^{\mu}
, and

\pi^{\mu\nu}
.

The 14-moment approximation specifies the

form of the distribution function
in terms of the dissipative currents, but
not
their equations of
motion. Nevertheless, once (
4.96
) is adopted, any moment of the
Boltzmann equation can be used to derive a closed set of relaxation-type
equations for
\Pi
,
V^{\mu}
, and
\pi^{\mu\nu}

[
30
,
31
]
. The general structure of
these equations will be the same in every case, but the numerical values of the
resulting transport coefficients depend on which moment is chosen for the
projection
[
31
]
. With the truncation now established, the
remaining task is to extract these equations of motion explicitly.

4.5
Hydrodynamic equations from kinetic theory

Once the local equilibrium state has been specified and the
non-equilibrium correction has been truncated through the 14-moment
approximation, the evolution equations for the dissipative currents are
derived by projecting the linearized Boltzmann equation

k^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{k}=\hat{L}[\phi]

(4.99)

onto each irreducible tensor sector. Multiplying both sides by

E_{k}^{r}\,k^{\langle\mu_{1}}\cdots k^{\mu_{\ell}\rangle}
and integrating
over the Lorentz-invariant momentum measure
dK
generates an exact
equation of motion for each irreducible moment

\rho_{r}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}
. The projected comoving derivative and collision integral are defined as
[
31
]

\dot{\rho}_{r}^{\langle\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}\rangle}\equiv\Delta^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}_{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{\ell}}\,u^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\rho_{r}^{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{\ell}},\qquad\mathcal{C}_{r}^{\langle\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}\rangle}\equiv\Delta^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}_{\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{\ell}}\int dK\,E_{k}^{r}\,k^{\nu_{1}}\cdots k^{\nu_{\ell}}\,\hat{L}[\phi].

(4.100)

Using the decomposition
k^{\mu}=E_{k}u^{\mu}+k^{\langle\mu\rangle}

and substituting the Boltzmann equation in the form

\delta\dot{f}_{k}=-\dot{f}_{0k}-E_{k}^{-1}k^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}f_{0k}-E_{k}^{-1}k^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\delta f_{k}+E_{k}^{-1}\hat{L}[\phi]
,
one obtains, after a lengthy but straightforward calculation, the
following exact equations of motion for the moments up to tensor rank
two
[
31
]
:

Scalar sector (
\ell=0
):

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{r}-\mathcal{C}_{r-1}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{r}^{(0)}\theta-\frac{G_{2r}}{D_{20}}\,\Pi\,\theta+\frac{G_{2r}}{D_{20}}\,\pi^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}+\frac{G_{3r}}{D_{20}}\,\nabla_{\mu}V^{\mu}+r\,\rho_{r-1}^{\mu}\dot{u}_{\mu}

(4.101)

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\mu}\rho_{r-1}^{\mu}+(r-1)\,\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{3}\bigl[(r+2)\,\rho_{r}-(r-1)\,m_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{r-2}\bigr]\theta,

Vector sector (
\ell=1
):

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{r}^{\langle\mu\rangle}-\mathcal{C}_{r-1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{r}^{(1)}\nabla^{\mu}\alpha_{0}+\rho_{r}^{\nu}\,\omega^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+\frac{1}{3}\bigl[(r-1)\,m_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{r-2}^{\mu}-(r+3)\,\rho_{r}^{\mu}\bigr]\theta

(4.102)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5}\bigl[(2r-2)\,m_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{r-2}^{\nu}-(2r+3)\,\rho_{r}^{\nu}\bigr]\sigma^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+r\,\rho_{r-1}^{\mu\nu}\dot{u}_{\nu}+(r-1)\,\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu\lambda}\sigma_{\lambda\nu}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}\bigl[m_{0}^{2}\,r\,\rho_{r-1}-(r+3)\,\rho_{r+1}\bigr]\dot{u}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{3}\nabla^{\mu}\bigl[m_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{r-1}-\rho_{r+1}\bigr]

\displaystyle-\Delta^{\mu}_{\ \lambda}\nabla_{\nu}\rho_{r-1}^{\lambda\nu}+\frac{\beta_{0}J_{r+2,1}}{\varepsilon_{0}+P_{0}}\bigl[\Pi\,\dot{u}^{\mu}-\nabla^{\mu}\Pi+\Delta^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\partial_{\lambda}\pi^{\lambda\nu}\bigr],

Tensor sector (
\ell=2
):

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{r}^{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}-\mathcal{C}_{r-1}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\alpha_{r}^{(2)}\sigma^{\mu\nu}+2\rho_{r}^{\lambda\langle\mu}\omega^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \lambda}-\frac{2}{7}\bigl[(2r+5)\,\rho_{r}^{\lambda\langle\mu}-2m_{0}^{2}(r-1)\,\rho_{r-2}^{\lambda\langle\mu}\bigr]\sigma^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \lambda}

(4.103)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{15}\bigl[(r+4)\,\rho_{r+2}-(2r+3)\,m_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{r}+(r-1)\,m_{0}^{4}\,\rho_{r-2}\bigr]\sigma^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{5}\nabla^{\langle\mu}\bigl[\rho_{r+1}^{\nu\rangle}-m_{0}^{2}\,\rho_{r-1}^{\nu\rangle}\bigr]-\frac{2}{5}\bigl[(r+5)\,\rho_{r+1}^{\langle\mu}-m_{0}^{2}\,r\,\rho_{r-1}^{\langle\mu}\bigr]\dot{u}^{\nu\rangle}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}\bigl[(r+4)\,\rho_{r}^{\mu\nu}-m_{0}^{2}(r-1)\,\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu}\bigr]\theta+r\,\rho_{r-1}^{\mu\nu\lambda}\dot{u}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle+(r-1)\,\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}\sigma_{\lambda\rho}-\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\lambda}\rho_{r-1}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}.

Here
\omega^{\mu\nu}\equiv(\nabla^{\mu}u^{\nu}-\nabla^{\nu}u^{\mu})/2

is the vorticity tensor,
I^{\mu}\equiv\nabla^{\mu}\alpha_{0}
, and

\alpha_{r}^{(0)}
,
\alpha_{r}^{(1)}
,
\alpha_{r}^{(2)}
are
thermodynamic functions given by equilibrium momentum integrals,

\displaystyle\alpha_{r}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=(1-r)\,\mathcal{I}_{r,1}-\mathcal{I}_{r,0}-\frac{G_{2r}(\varepsilon_{0}+P_{0})-G_{3r}n_{0}}{D_{20}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{r}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{r+1,1}-h_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{J}_{r+2,1},\qquad\alpha_{r}^{(2)}=\mathcal{I}_{r+2,1}+(r-1)\,\mathcal{I}_{r+2,2},

(4.104)

with
G_{nm}\equiv\mathcal{J}_{n0}\mathcal{J}_{m0}-\mathcal{J}_{n-1,0}\mathcal{J}_{m+1,0}
,

D_{20}\equiv\mathcal{J}_{10}\mathcal{J}_{30}-\mathcal{J}_{20}^{2}
,
and
h_{0}=(\varepsilon_{0}+P_{0})/n_{0}
the enthalpy per particle.
All comoving derivatives of
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
that appear have
been eliminated using the conservation laws for particle number,
energy, and momentum. Deriving these moment equations is also a fundamental part of this thesis. In the next Chapter, we shall revisit this problem in the context of the Boltzmann–Vlasov equation and derive them more carefully.

Equations (
4.101
)–(
4.103
)
constitute an infinite, exact hierarchy: the equation for

\rho_{r}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}
involves
\rho_{r}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell+1}}

(through the gradient
\nabla_{\lambda}\rho_{r-1}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}
),

\rho_{r\pm 1}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell\pm 1}}
(through velocity-gradient
couplings), and rank-
(\ell+2)
moments (through the
\sigma_{\lambda\rho}

and
\dot{u}_{\lambda}
terms). This hierarchy is still completely
equivalent to the Boltzmann equation.

4.5.1
Closure via the 14-moment approximation

The hierarchy is closed by imposing the 14-moment ansatz (
4.98
),

\rho_{r}=a_{r}\,\Pi,\qquad\rho_{r}^{\mu}=b_{r}\,V^{\mu},\qquad\rho_{r}^{\mu\nu}=c_{r}\,\pi^{\mu\nu},\qquad\rho_{r}^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{\ell}}=0\quad(\ell\geq 3),

(4.105)

with coefficients
a_{r}
,
b_{r}
,
c_{r}
given by equilibrium momentum integrals
through (
4.93
). The collision integrals are evaluated by inserting
the 14-moment distribution function (
4.96
) into the
definitions (
4.100
). For each sector this yields a term
proportional to the corresponding dissipative current,

\mathcal{C}_{r-1}=-\mathcal{A}_{r0}^{(0)}\,\Pi,\qquad\mathcal{C}_{r-1}^{\mu}=-\mathcal{A}_{r0}^{(1)}\,V^{\mu},\qquad\mathcal{C}_{r-1}^{\mu\nu}=-\mathcal{A}_{r0}^{(2)}\,\pi^{\mu\nu},

(4.106)

where
\mathcal{A}_{r0}^{(\ell)}
are the matrix elements of the linearised collision
operator projected onto the truncated basis
[
31
]
.
Substituting (
4.105
) and (
4.106
)
into (
4.101
)–(
4.103
) and retaining only the

r=0
moment in each sector (which correspond to
\rho_{0}=-(m^{2}/3)\Pi
,
\rho_{0}^{\mu}=V^{\mu}
and
\rho_{0}^{\mu\nu}=\pi^{\mu\nu}
), one obtains a closed set of
relaxation equations for the dissipative currents. Within the 14-moment approximation
the relaxation times and first-order transport coefficients are expressed directly in
terms of the collision matrix elements
\mathcal{A}_{00}^{(\ell)}
and the thermodynamic
integrals
\alpha_{0}^{(\ell)}
(
4.104
),

\tau_{\Pi}=\frac{m^{2}/3}{\mathcal{A}_{00}^{(0)}},\quad\zeta=-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{(0)}}{\mathcal{A}_{00}^{(0)}}\,\tau_{\Pi},\qquad\tau_{n}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}_{00}^{(1)}},\quad\kappa=\frac{\alpha_{0}^{(1)}}{\mathcal{A}_{00}^{(1)}}\,\tau_{n},\qquad\tau_{\pi}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}_{00}^{(2)}},\quad\eta=\frac{\alpha_{0}^{(2)}}{\mathcal{A}_{00}^{(2)}}\,\tau_{\pi}.

(4.107)

The resulting second-order relaxation equations
read
[
61
,
31
]

\tau_{\Pi}\,\dot{\Pi}+\Pi=-\zeta\,\theta-\ell_{\Pi V}\,\nabla_{\mu}V^{\mu}-\tau_{\Pi V}\,V^{\mu}\dot{u}_{\mu}-\delta_{\Pi\Pi}\,\Pi\,\theta-\lambda_{\Pi V}\,V^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\alpha_{0}+\lambda_{\Pi\pi}\,\pi^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu},

(4.108)

\displaystyle\tau_{n}\,\dot{V}^{\langle\mu\rangle}+V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\kappa\,\nabla^{\mu}\alpha_{0}-V_{\nu}\,\omega^{\nu\mu}-\delta_{nn}\,V^{\mu}\,\theta-\ell_{n\Pi}\,\nabla^{\mu}\Pi+\ell_{n\pi}\,\Delta^{\mu}_{\ \lambda}\partial_{\nu}\pi^{\lambda\nu}

(4.109)

\displaystyle+\tau_{n\Pi}\,\Pi\,\dot{u}^{\mu}-\tau_{n\pi}\,\pi^{\mu\nu}\dot{u}_{\nu}-\lambda_{nn}\,V_{\nu}\sigma^{\mu\nu}+\lambda_{n\Pi}\,\Pi\,\nabla^{\mu}\alpha_{0}-\lambda_{n\pi}\,\pi^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\alpha_{0},

\displaystyle\tau_{\pi}\,\dot{\pi}^{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}+\pi^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\eta\,\sigma^{\mu\nu}+2\pi^{\lambda\langle\mu}\omega^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \lambda}-\delta_{\pi\pi}\,\pi^{\mu\nu}\theta-\tau_{\pi\pi}\,\pi^{\lambda\langle\mu}\sigma^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \lambda}+\lambda_{\pi\Pi}\,\Pi\,\sigma^{\mu\nu}

(4.110)

\displaystyle-\tau_{\pi n}\,V^{\langle\mu}\dot{u}^{\nu\rangle}+\ell_{\pi n}\,\nabla^{\langle\mu}V^{\nu\rangle}+\lambda_{\pi n}\,V^{\langle\mu}\nabla^{\nu\rangle}\alpha_{0}.

As before,
\dot{A}\equiv u^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}A
,

\theta\equiv\partial_{\mu}u^{\mu}
,

\nabla^{\mu}\equiv\Delta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}
,

\alpha_{0}\equiv\mu/T
, and

\sigma^{\mu\nu}\equiv\nabla^{\langle\mu}u^{\nu\rangle}
.
The coefficients
\zeta
,
\kappa
,
\eta
are the bulk viscosity, particle diffusion, and shear viscosity, given by (
4.107
).
The second-order coefficients
\delta_{\Pi\Pi}
,
\ell_{\Pi V}
,
\tau_{\Pi V}
,

\lambda_{\Pi V}
,
\lambda_{\Pi\pi}
,
\delta_{nn}
,
\ell_{n\Pi}
,
\ell_{n\pi}
,

\tau_{n\Pi}
,
\tau_{n\pi}
,
\lambda_{nn}
,
\lambda_{n\Pi}
,
\lambda_{n\pi}
,

\delta_{\pi\pi}
,
\tau_{\pi\pi}
,
\tau_{\pi n}
,
\ell_{\pi n}
,

\lambda_{\pi\Pi}
, and
\lambda_{\pi n}
encode the couplings among the three
dissipative sectors and are likewise determined by equilibrium momentum integrals and
collision matrix elements
[
31
]
. In contrast to relativistic
Navier–Stokes theory
[
38
,
72
]
, the dissipative currents
relax toward their Navier–Stokes values over finite microscopic timescales

\tau_{\Pi}
,
\tau_{n}
,
\tau_{\pi}
set by the collision kernel.

The complete hydrodynamic description is obtained by supplementing the conservation
laws
\partial_{\mu}N^{\mu}=0
and
\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0

with (
4.108
)–(
4.110
), giving a closed,
causal, transient theory of relativistic dissipative
hydrodynamics
[
61
,
31
]
.

4.6
Electromagnetic fields

When electromagnetic fields are present, the relativistic fluid framework must be extended
to magnetohydrodynamics: the fluid interacts with the field through the Lorentz force and
Maxwell’s equations, while dissipative effects require a second-order Israel–Stewart-type
formulation
[
62
,
61
]
to avoid the acausality and linear instabilities
that afflict the relativistic Navier–Stokes description
[
57
,
58
]
.
In the non-resistive limit, where the electric conductivity is taken to infinity so that the
electric field vanishes in the local rest frame, the magnetic field still leaves a non-trivial
imprint on the dissipative structure, modifying the tensorial structure of the transport
coefficients and appearing explicitly in the second-order relaxation equations
[
33
]
.

The most straightforward way to derive magnetohydrodynamic equations of motion is to simply repeat the procedure outlined in the previous section for the Boltzmann-Vlasov equation. This is the approach developed in Refs.
[
33
,
34
]
. This approach has a fundamental flaw: it describes a single-component gas of charged particles and thus can never achieve local charge neutrality – as a consequence, this system can never achieve global equilibrium. Nevertheless, it serves as an interesting test case to derive the equations and understand their possible mathematical structure.

At the microscopic level, charged particles experience the Lorentz force, and the kinetic
equation becomes the Boltzmann–Vlasov equation
[
28
,
23
]
,

k^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{\mathbf{k}}+q\,F^{\mu\nu}k_{\nu}\frac{\partial f_{\mathbf{k}}}{\partial k^{\mu}}=C[f],

(4.111)

where the second term on the left-hand side describes the acceleration of particles in
momentum space due to the Lorentz force. The Lorentz is given in terms of the Faraday tensor,
F^{\mu\nu}
, which we express in terms of
u^{\mu}
,

F^{\mu\nu}=E^{\mu}u^{\nu}-E^{\nu}u^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,u_{\alpha}B_{\beta},

(4.112)

with
E^{\mu}
and
B^{\mu}
being the electric and magnetic fields in the local rest frame of the fluid,

E^{\mu}\equiv F^{\mu\nu}u_{\nu},\qquad B^{\mu}\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}\,u_{\nu},\qquad E^{\mu}u_{\mu}=0,\qquad B^{\mu}u_{\mu}=0.

(4.113)

The collision term is the same one used in the previous section and does not contain any effects due to the electromagnetic fields.

Repeating the steps outlined in the previous section and taking moments of Eq. (
4.111
) with weight

E_{\mathbf{k}}^{r}k^{\langle\mu_{1}}\cdots k^{\mu_{\ell}\rangle}
generates once more an infinite hierarchy
of evolution equations
[
32
,
33
]
. The structure of this hierarchy
mirrors the field-free case, but each sector acquires additional source terms proportional to

F^{\mu\nu}
. The key results are as follows.

Scalar moments (
\ell=0
):

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{r}-\mathcal{C}_{r-1}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{r}^{(0)}\theta+\frac{G_{3r}}{D_{20}}\partial_{\mu}V_{f}^{\mu}+\frac{\theta}{3}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}(r-1)\rho_{r-2}-(r+2)\rho_{r}-3\frac{G_{2r}}{D_{20}}\Pi\right]+r\rho_{r-1}^{\mu}\dot{u}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla_{\mu}\rho_{r-1}^{\mu}+\!\left[(r-1)\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{G_{2r}}{D_{20}}\pi^{\mu\nu}\right]\!\sigma_{\mu\nu}-\frac{G_{2r}}{D_{20}}\,qE_{\nu}V_{f}^{\nu}-(r-1)\,qE_{\nu}\rho_{r-2}^{\nu}.

(4.114)

The last two terms are new relative to the field-free result
[
32
]
and
encode the coupling of the electric field to the diffusion current and to lower-rank moments.
In the non-resistive limit
E^{\mu}=0
these terms vanish and the scalar sector is
unaffected by the electromagnetic field.

Vector moments (
\ell=1
):

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{r}^{\langle\mu\rangle}-\mathcal{C}_{r-1}^{\langle\mu\rangle}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{r}^{(1)}\nabla^{\mu}\alpha_{0}+r\rho_{r-1}^{\mu\nu}\dot{u}_{\nu}-\frac{1}{3}\nabla^{\mu}\!\left(m_{0}^{2}\rho_{r-1}-\rho_{r+1}\right)-\Delta^{\mu}_{\ \alpha}\!\left(\nabla_{\nu}\rho_{r-1}^{\alpha\nu}+\alpha_{r}^{h}\partial_{\kappa}\pi^{\kappa\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{3}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}(r-1)\rho_{r-2}^{\mu}-(r+3)\rho_{r}^{\mu}\right]\!\theta+(r-1)\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu\lambda}\sigma_{\nu\lambda}+\rho_{r,\nu}\omega^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{5}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}(2r-2)\rho_{r-2,\nu}-(2r+3)\rho_{r,\nu}\right]+\alpha_{r}^{h}\nabla^{\mu}\Pi

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{3}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}r\rho_{r-1}-(r+3)\rho_{r+1}-3\alpha_{r}^{h}\Pi\right]\!\dot{u}^{\mu}-\alpha_{r}^{h}\,qB\,b^{\mu}_{\ \nu}V_{f}^{\nu}-qB\,b^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\rho_{r-1}^{\nu}

\displaystyle\quad+\!\left(\alpha_{r}^{h}n_{f0}+\beta_{0}J_{r+1,1}\right)qE^{\mu}+\frac{1}{3}\!\left[(r+2)\rho_{r}-m_{0}^{2}(r-1)\rho_{r-2}\right]qE^{\mu}-(r-1)\,\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu}\,qE_{\nu}.

(4.115)

The terms proportional to
qB

are the magnetic Lorentz-force couplings and the terms proportional to
qE^{\mu}
are new to the resistive case and vanish when
E^{\mu}=0
.

Rank-two tensor moments (
\ell=2
):

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{r}^{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}-\mathcal{C}_{r-1}^{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}

\displaystyle=2\alpha_{r}^{(2)}\sigma^{\mu\nu}+\frac{2}{15}\!\left[m_{0}^{4}(r-1)\rho_{r-2}-(2r+3)m_{0}^{2}\rho_{r}+(r+4)\rho_{r+2}\right]\!\sigma^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{5}\dot{u}^{\langle\mu}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}r\rho_{r-1}^{\nu\rangle}-(r+5)\rho_{r+1}^{\nu\rangle}\right]-\frac{2}{5}\nabla^{\langle\mu}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}\rho_{r-1}^{\nu\rangle}-\rho_{r+1}^{\nu\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+r\rho_{r-1}^{\mu\nu\gamma}\dot{u}_{\gamma}-\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\lambda}\rho_{r-1}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}+(r-1)\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu\lambda\kappa}\sigma_{\lambda\kappa}+2\rho_{r}^{\lambda\langle\mu}\omega^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \lambda}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{3}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}(r-1)\rho_{r-2}^{\mu\nu}-(r+4)\rho_{r}^{\mu\nu}\right]\!\theta+\frac{2}{7}\!\left[m_{0}^{2}(2r-2)\rho_{r-2}^{\kappa\langle\mu}-(2r+5)\rho_{r}^{\kappa\langle\mu}\right]\!\sigma^{\nu\rangle}_{\ \kappa}

\displaystyle\quad-2\,qB\,b^{\alpha\beta}\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\ \ \alpha\kappa}g_{\lambda\beta}\rho_{r-1}^{\kappa\lambda}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,qE^{\langle\mu}\rho_{r}^{\nu\rangle}-(r-1)\,\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\!\left[qE_{\lambda}\rho_{r-2}^{\alpha\beta\lambda}+\frac{2}{5}\,qE^{\langle\alpha}\!\left(m_{0}^{2}\rho_{r-2}^{\beta\rangle}-\rho_{r}^{\beta\rangle}\right)\right].

(4.116)

The term proportional to
qB
is due to the magnetic Lorentz-force and the remaining terms proportional to
qE^{\mu}
are due to the electric Lorentz force and vanish in the non-resistive limit.

As before, this infinite moment hierarchy is closed by the 14-moment
approximation (
4.108
)– (
4.110
), which
is essentially unmodified by the inclusion of electromagnetic fields. Replacing these relations into the moment equations for
r=0
, one simply obtains:

Bulk viscous pressure

\displaystyle\tau_{\Pi}\dot{\Pi}+\Pi

\displaystyle=-\zeta\,\theta-\ell_{\Pi V}\nabla_{\mu}V_{f}^{\mu}-\tau_{\Pi V}V_{f}^{\mu}\dot{u}_{\mu}-\delta_{\Pi\Pi}\Pi\,\theta-\lambda_{\Pi V}V_{f}^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\alpha_{0}+\lambda_{\Pi\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}-\delta_{\Pi VE}\,q\,V_{f}^{\mu}E_{\mu},

(4.117)

with relaxation time
